Test (*Mac only*): "endless cursors of duplicate or invalid records end at the caps". |
| AD4 | Medium-Low | One broken object stopped the whole sync: a missing asset (`fileURL` nil, `assetFileNotFound`, `assetNotAvailable`) and a failed coordinated read of one file mapped to `unavailable`, and the engine publishes only when the transport was available, so one record A1 broke kept this device from publishing indefinitely. | **Fixed.** `SyncTransportError.objectUnreadable` (new, not `stopsSync`): that object is refused alone and its stream waits at it. Tests: "one unreadable object is refused alone; the device still publishes" (adversarial "sync log", Linux), "one record with a missing asset is refused alone" (*Mac only*). |
| AD5 | Low | `NSFileCoordinator` had no timeout or cancellation, so a writer holding coordination (the iCloud daemon, another app, a hostile presenter) held a sync open; and the download check ran outside coordination (a file evicted in between was read anyway). | **Fixed.** Each access is a `CoordinationAttempt` actor that owns its coordinator, gives up at a deadline (30 s, raised to two minutes after AD11) or on cancellation and cancels the coordinator; a fetch that times out is `objectUnreadable`. The check runs again inside the accessor. Test (*Mac only*): "a read not granted coordination before its deadline is given up". |
| AD6 | Low | `conflictingVersions` refuses an iCloud Drive object with conflict versions, which leaves that device's stream waiting at it for good; iCloud may also rename a second create of one name to `N 2.wwe`, which the layout ignores, so that copy is never read. Neither is resolved. | **Accepted, for the owner** (architecture §7.3, implementation plan milestone 3): keeping "the copy that verifies" needs the engine's signature check and is an owner decision. Both cases need a device to reuse a sequence number, which the engine prevents unless its listing lags the store. |
| AD7 | Low | `URLSessionHTTPClient`: `UInt64(totalTimeout * 1e9)` trapped on an infinite or huge limit; URLSession sent its default `User-Agent` when a request named none and the device's languages in `Accept-Language`; system and MDM proxies were not mentioned; the decompression limit was claimed though untested; the timeout tests waited on the wall clock. | **Fixed.** Limits are clamped to 1 ms to 600 s (NaN included); a default `User-Agent: Watchword` and a fixed `Accept-Language: *`; §13.8 notes proxies (a TLS-intercepting one the device trusts sees the prefix) and that the decompression limit is untested through stubs; the deadline is injected, so the stalled and dripping servers are tested with a deadline already passed. Tests (*Mac only*): "time limits are held to their range", the "slow" cases, "a request that names no User-Agent". |
| AD8 | Low | `FileDeviceLocalStore` checked only the last component for links (a link above the store directory redirected it), removed a planted link and then wrote by path (a link planted in between was followed), set 0700 only at creation, and marked backup exclusion per file. | **Fixed.** The store is `components` below a trusted ancestor (the App Group container, links resolved once); every component is checked at every operation and created one at a time. Writes go to an exclusively created temporary file (`withoutOverwriting`), moved into place after removing whatever was under the name, so no write follows a link. 0700 and the directory's backup exclusion (which covers its contents) are set again before every write. Tests (Linux): "a link anywhere on the path below the container is refused", "an existing directory is made owner-only again". |
| AD9 | Low (test) | `FakeCloudKitDatabase` evaluated the `CKQuery`'s predicate, which raises `NSInternalInconsistencyException` ("This predicate has evaluation disabled") on Apple platforms and aborted the adversarial process, hiding every suite after it. | **Fixed.** The fake reads the predicate (`TRUEPREDICATE`, or `name BEGINSWITH <string>`) and records an issue and throws for any other shape, so a changed query cannot pass unnoticed. It stays a copy per test target: shared in `WatchwordTestSupport` it would need `@unchecked Sendable` under `Sources/`, which the unsafe tripwire forbids. |
| AD10 | Low (test) | "files of other names are never records" planted the lowercase spelling of the record's own name, which macOS's case-insensitive APFS treats as the record itself. | **Fixed.** That variant is planted only where a probe shows the volume is case-sensitive; a new test pins that a record has one file name (its canonical `uuidString`) and that on a case-insensitive volume a case variant is that same file. |
| AD11 | Low | The first full macOS adversarial run (Xcode 27, three cores, `swift test --parallel`) failed five of the adapters' tests at the same moment, about 110 s in: two HTTP cases with URLSession's own `-1001` timeout instead of `responseTooLarge`, and the first coordinated call of three iCloud Drive cases with `unavailable`. Both are 30-second wall-clock timers (URLSession's resource timeout, or its 15-second idle timeout, and the coordination deadline), so the likeliest cause is a stretch in which the loaded runner scheduled none of these tests' work (the URLSession read loop and the actors around coordination) rather than five logic faults. The tests also planted "sparse" files of 8 GiB and 4 GiB, which cost real disk traffic on any file system that does not keep them sparse. Not reproduced: it needs a Mac. | **Fixed in the tests, hardened in production.** The adapters' suites are `.serialized`, every test that is not about a timeout uses URLSession timers at their 600-second ceiling and an overall or coordination deadline no test reaches (each deadline keeps a test of its own, with a deadline injected as already passed), the URL-loading stub delivers bodies in 16 KiB pieces so the limit is met while bytes are still arriving, and the oversized files are 128 and 256 MiB, still well over the limits. In production the coordination deadline is two minutes, counted only while coordination has not been granted: a granted access is never given up, however late its task is scheduled. The HTTP client's limits stay at 15 s idle and 30 s overall, since a scan is retried and nothing is lost; a device slow enough to miss them only checks later. The same tests also run in the package-tests job, whose log showed no failure; they were exposed to the same timers there. |

## Q. Review of the account screens (`WatchwordPresentation` and the SwiftUI views)

Review date: 2026-11-16, an independent security review of the screens added for milestones 3, 4, 6 and 7, against the
adversaries of architecture §2 who reach the person through what a screen shows: **A1** (the store and the key
directory), **A4** (a member who may write, or a device someone else enrolled), **A7** (whoever hands over an import
file) and anyone who can send the person a message. Fixed in a remote Linux session; the views and XCUITests are not
yet built on a Mac for these fixes. Findings 1 and 8's confirmation were reproduced by tests that failed before the
fix (`AdversarialSharedVaultTests` "after a relaunch…", "an invitation from a verified owner asks before joining");
the rest were fixed with their tests written alongside.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Q1 | High | After a relaunch every member showed as verified. `keyCheck` was set only by `ingestMembership`, so a restored vault had none, `SharedVaultStatus` reported nobody as substituted or unverified, and the member list took "in no list" for verified: a member whose keys A1 had swapped lost the "keys changed" warning (D17, SEC3), and the sync keyring's S39 check had nothing to refuse by until the next sync. | **Fixed.** The check is made when a vault is restored at unlock and whenever the status finds it stale; `SharedVaultStatus.verifiedMembers` is the explicit list (`nil` with no check on the current head), and the screen shows "not checked yet" for anyone it does not name (§8.4). `AdversarialSharedVaultTests` "after a relaunch a member whose keys differ…", "Screen models: member trust". |
| Q2 | High | A pasted `WWFP1-…` code hid the typed-number field and the comparison toggle, and one tap pinned the contact as `.scannedCode`. Anyone who can message the person can send a code for an account of their own. The role step also said "Safety numbers match" whatever had happened. | **Fixed.** A pasted code only names the account; a typed number or the person's own comparison is still required, and the pin records which. `.scannedCode` is never recorded until the app can scan with the camera. The role step names the account and says how its keys were confirmed (`docs/05-anti-phishing.md` §3). "Adversarial: account screens". |
| Q3 | Medium | The merge's host switches showed the host as the file spelled it, without its reach or exclusions, and never ran `PhishingDetector`: an A7 file could offer an existing item `аmazon.com` (Cyrillic `а`), identical on screen to the real one. | **Fixed.** `MergeHost`: ASCII (punycode) form, the registrable domain in bold, "and its subdomains" and the exclusions, `PhishingDetector` against the vault's items, a warning naming the site, and a second confirmation to accept a look-alike or another alphabet. Punycode known answers from Python. "Screen models: hosts a merge would add", "Adversarial: account screens". |
| Q4 | Medium | A new item could land in a shared vault that an editing member (A4) had renamed "Personal": the list's filter, the editor and the default destination showed names alone. | **Fixed.** Every list labels vaults by ownership and role (`VaultRow.badge`), the editor names the destination with "everyone in this vault can see it" for a shared one. Renaming stays any writer's (§8.4, "Names of shared vaults": an owner-only rename would need every device's judge to enforce it); for the owner to confirm. |
| Q5 | Low | The export relied on `fileExporter` and `FileWrapper.fileAttributes` for file protection, and the plaintext bytes stayed in an array until the screen went. | **Fixed.** The app writes the file itself with `.completeFileProtectionUnlessOpen` (iOS) into a folder of its own, hands it to the file mover and removes what is left; the bytes are `SecureBytes`, zeroised once saved, on cancel, on close and at lock. macOS has no protection classes (§14.1). Whether the mover keeps the attribute across providers is unverified. |
| Q6 | Low | The models' secrets (the merge's values and what was revealed, the export's bytes, typed passwords) went only when their views were torn down. | **Fixed.** `VaultSession.lockEvents()` and `LockSignal`: every lock, however caused, makes each `LockSensitive` model drop them; the removal form moved into `DevicesModel` for the same reason. Model tests over a real lock, and `ImportExportTests` (a lock with the merge review open, and with the export report open). |
| Q7 | Low | `QuarantinedVersion.id` could repeat for two kept versions at one stamp; every shared-vault version got §8.7's "held aside" explanation, U5's implausible ones included; and a version's title was looked up by record ID in any vault. | **Fixed.** The ID covers the key ID and a digest of the bytes; `QuarantineReason` (derived, since the state store keeps no reason: beyond the jump allowed above what the vault holds is U5's, other shared-vault versions the judge's, the rest Y37's), each with its own explanation; a title only from an item of the same vault. Storing the reason with the entry is left for a state-store format change. |
| Q8 | Low | Device names are chosen by whoever enrolled the device, and the screen appended "(this device)" to the name text, so a name could claim it, or reverse the text around it. Accepting an invitation from an owner pinned earlier joined at once. | **Fixed.** Names are cleaned of control, formatting and separator characters and of any "(this device)"; this device is marked from its ID, apart from the name, and sentences quote names. Accepting always asks before joining. |

## Z. End-to-end audit of 2026-11-17 (whole codebase, by attack surface)

Audit date: 2026-11-17, of `main` at `5ba9d95`: eight read-only auditors, one per attack surface, each followed by a
sceptical verifier. Nothing was run during the audit; a finding called confirmed there means two readers agreed. The
fixes were made afterwards in a remote Linux session, each from a reproduction that failed first where one could be
written. Severities are the verifier's.

### Account keys, unlocking and the device registry (Z1 to Z19)

The adversaries are those of section J and of architecture §2: **a device the person revoked with a password change**
(its holder opened the keyset while it was the person's, so holds the account key, the device's own key, the Secret
Key and the password just retired, and can still write to the sync store), **a registered device in hands that know
a retired password**, **A8** with the local database of a locked device, and **A3** with the unlocked device and its
passcode. Each finding was reproduced first: the tests named below were run, cut down to the interfaces of the code
as audited, against a copy of `485baac`, and failed there for the reason the row gives. Z8 was found while fixing Z1.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Z1 | High | A password change made with the password the person still types was adopted silently at unlock. On a device not yet given a new password, `openAccount` tried every pending candidate header with the typed password *before* the device's own header, and consulted `ignoringPasswordChange` only afterwards; `adopt` asked nothing about what the candidate's branch did. The revoked device publishes, back-dated to before its revocation, a revocation of the device that revoked it and a credential entry whose header is derived from the retired password under a new salt. Typing the retired password there, with or without the flag, followed that branch for good: the revoker revoked, the revoked device active, the genuine change dead behind the new checkpoint, the retired password accepted again. The same device could keep the genuine change from being adopted at all: three headers of higher generations, each under parameters of its choosing up to the 25M-iteration ceiling, used up the three derivations of an unlock (and of an enrolment) ahead of the genuine header, so the new password reported as wrong. And a change whose own history revoked the adopting device was adopted like any other. W7 and architecture §7.8 promised that a lagging device "refuses the old password once it holds that header". | **Fixed.** The device's own header is tried first, and nothing derived under another's parameters runs ahead of it. If the password opens it while a change is pending, it is the retired one: `passwordChangedOnAnotherDevice`, never an adoption. With `ignoringPasswordChange` it unlocks and no candidate is read; with the flag and a password that does not open the device's own header, nothing is tried. Otherwise each device that published a pending change gets one attempt, on its newest header (`candidateHeaders`, one per author; a sync fetches and keeps one per author; enrolment spends one derivation per authoring device), so a chain of changes under one device's name costs one. A candidate whose branch revokes this device is refused (`passwordChangeRevokesThisDevice`). When more devices have published a change than an unlock tries and none of those tried opens, the unlock says so (`tooManyPasswordChangesPending`) and the person names the device from an unlocked session (`pendingPasswordChanges`, which shows who made each change and what it would revoke; `followPasswordChange(by:password:)`, which fetches that device's header if no sync kept it; it now takes the current password too, Z14, and every author is listed, Z13). Suite "account enrolment and device registry": `retiredPasswordFork`, `plantedChain` (unlock and enrolment), `changeThatRevokesThisDevice`, `moreAuthorsThanAttempts`. **Left:** to a device that holds no newer password, the genuine change and the forged one are symmetric, so which is the person's remains theirs to say; the order in which authors are tried is no defence and is documented as none. Enrolment cannot check a header's author against a registry it does not have yet, so a store that plants headers under device IDs of its invention still exhausts enrolment's budget (W5, Y7: it can as easily delete the real one), and a new device given the retired password enrols into a fork made with it, where nothing it writes counts on any device that follows the genuine change. *Mac only*: the Devices screen's model lists the changes and follows the one named (`DevicesModel.passwordChanges`, `follow`; "Screen models: a password changed by someone else" `followTheChange`), and the unlock screen's wording for the new errors is in `ErrorWording`; no view shows either yet. |
| Z2 | High | The verifier's addition to Z1. `DeviceRegistry.evaluate` looked at sixteen candidate credential entries in entry-ID order and counted each *before* the replay that judged it (`maximumCandidates`, untested and undocumented). The revoked device needs no password for this: sixteen credential entries with any signature in the "previous generation" slot, ground to sort below the genuine change, and the genuine change was never looked at. It was not pending, its header was never fetched, `isAwaitingNewPassword` was false, the old password kept unlocking and sync kept merging and publishing: W1's outcome again, by another road. | **Fixed.** Credential entries off the followed chain are *listed* from their own signatures and their links back to the chain and nothing else (author's signature under a key an ancestor registers, the previous generation's enrolment key, consecutive generations, a `previous` that is followed or itself listed, and not an entry that can never count on this chain): one verification per entry, no replay, so no budget to exhaust; `maximumCandidates` is gone. Whether a listed change can be *followed* is still decided by replaying with it as the last checkpoint (`evaluate(credential:)`), which is what adoption does. The list can therefore name a change that would not replay; making one takes the account key, a device key and the replaced generation's enrolment key, and whoever holds those can make one that does. `plantedChangesCannotHideTheGenuineOne` (96 planted entries over four seeds, asserting that sixteen sort first; before: the genuine change absent in every seed); "Device registry" `puppets` now asserts the listed-but-unfollowable case. |
| Z3 | Medium | That a password change was pending was remembered only in the local file. The refusal of the retired password, and of quick unlock, asked whether a *candidate header row* was held; the Keychain anchor pins the heads of valid entries on the followed chain, and a pending credential entry is neither. A8 with the retired password deleted the header row (enough to unlock and to keep quick unlock), or that and the credential entry (sync merged and published again), or any registry row the anchor names (the registry could not be evaluated, so nothing was pending). With no file write at all, a transport that delivered the registry entry and withheld the header (A1, Y7) left a device that reported `awaitingNewPassword` and opened with the old password. W15 had moved "which credentials do I follow" into the Keychain; this was the same promise one step earlier. | **Fixed.** The anchor (`WWRA2`) records the pending credential entries seen while the device follows a given credential entry, and keeps them until it follows another. `passwordChangePending` is asked of the anchor first and of the registry's `pendingCredentials` second, never of header rows, and a registry that cannot be evaluated hides nothing the anchor recorded; unlock, quick unlock, `isAwaitingNewPassword` and sync's merge-and-publish gate all ask it. `pendingChangeOutlivesTheFile`, four variants (header row; header and credential rows; a registry row the anchor names; header withheld on the transport). |
| Z4 | Low | Two more lock-during-await traps of W22's kind. After a password unlock installed its keys, `refreshQuickUnlock` waited on the Keychain and `refreshSigningKey` then read the keyset with no check; after a quick unlock the same read followed the write of the quick-unlock state; and `adoptVaultKey` took the keys, awaited a store read, then wrapped under the keyset. A `lock()` in any of those waits (backgrounding locks at once by default) zeroised the keyset, and reading a zeroised key traps by design: a crash from an auto-lock, an AutoFill extension dying mid-request. No key or data exposed. | **Fixed.** `refreshSigningKey` checks the keyset before reading it, as `dualSignature` and `signedRegistryEntry` do; `adoptVaultKey` makes both store reads first and takes the keys with nothing suspending before the wrap; the pins a shared vault now saves are signed before the first write of a save. Suite "Vault session: a lock during the account's objects": `lockWhileUnlockWaitsOnTheKeychain`, `lockWhileQuickUnlockWritesItsState`, `lockWhileAdoptingAVaultKey` (each ended in the precondition trap before). |
| Z5 | High | The Personal vault's identity was taken from the unauthenticated account row. `personal_vault_id` and `personal_vault_key_id` are plain columns; an unlock required only that *some* resident key had the ID the row named, and compared the header's AUK-sealed binding with the row only as a condition for re-pinning the anchor, skipping quietly on a mismatch. A member of a vault shared with the victim (A4) who gets one write to the victim's database while the app is locked (A8, A3 on a Mac, an altered backup restored) names that vault as Personal and plants a directory row that keeps the real one in view. From the next unlock, with the victim's own password or biometric, every new item, every login AutoFill saves and every new passkey is sealed under a key the attacker holds and published to that vault's log; a later password change on that device publishes the other vault as Personal in the header. Architecture §6 said an edited column only makes a row unreadable. The victim must be able to write to the vault; otherwise saving fails and nothing leaks. | **Fixed.** At every unlock, by password or by quick unlock, once the keyset has opened and before any key is installed, the stored header must be this account record's own and its binding must open under the Account Unlock Key to the vault the row names (`requirePersonalVaultBinding`); the key the row names must be a key of that vault and not one that arrived by another member's grant; and the comparison after unlock is a hard failure too. All of them `VaultError.personalVaultMismatch`, with the session left locked. A missing header row is refused wherever the Keychain holds a registry anchor. `personalVaultIsTheHeadersNotTheRows` (row redirected; that and the header row removed; the header row replaced by another account's, relabelled; before: unlocked each time, by quick unlock too, with the other vault as the default), `grantedKeyIsNeverThePersonalKey`. **Left:** a store with neither header row nor anchor (an account from before the registry, or a backup restored onto other hardware with its header removed) is still taken at the row's word for a vault whose key is the account's own; such a store has no registry until its first sync starts one, and the header it makes then seals the row's vault (corrected by Z16). Directory rows are still unauthenticated as to *which* vaults exist; they add no key. |
| Z6 | High | Other members followed whichever branch of a member's device registry had the highest credential generation. An account's own devices pin the genesis entry and follow the change the person's password opened; for another member's registry there is neither, and `evaluateMemberRegistry` started at the genesis and took `pendingCredentials.last` until none was left. A member's laptop is stolen unlocked and revoked with a password change; its holder still has the account key, the laptop's key and the previous generation's enrolment key, and publishes a competing change from that generation, sealing a back-dated revocation of the member's phone, and one more on top. Every other member's device then held the laptop registered and the phone revoked: the laptop wrote as the member in every vault they share, and the member's real devices were no longer read. Cheaper still, one more genesis entry under the member's account key made the function return nothing ("exactly one"), and after a relaunch nothing the member signed was read. §8.10 said devices revoked from a member's account are not read. | **Fixed as far as entries alone allow; the rest tracked.** To an outsider the two branches are symmetric, so no rule picks one. Each member's registry is anchored on this device (the genesis entry and the credential entry followed; signed by this account and saved with each vault that lists the member, `WWMD`) and moves only forward, one credential entry at a time, where exactly one change follows that replays as a chain. A change that leaves the chain at or before the anchored entry is never followed, whatever its generation. Where two or more follow it, neither is taken: only a device registered at the anchored entry *and in every competing branch* counts, so neither the device a branch revokes nor the one it keeps signs anything that counts, while a device nobody contests carries on. A second genesis entry neither replaces nor removes the one anchored; with none anchored, two anchor neither. Every such sight is reported (`SharedVaultStatus.membersWithDisagreeingDevices`, worded "this person's devices disagree: verify with them again"), and only `verifyMemberDevices(of:code:)`, with the twelve-digit code the member's own device shows (`memberDevicesCode`), moves an anchor to a branch (removed by Z11: nothing in this version settles a contested registry; and Z12 makes a branch followed forward compete with a later sibling). "shared vaults through the session" `revokedDeviceOfAMemberForksTheRegistry` (the laptop's holder forks the member's registry and writes into the vault: the member who had followed the genuine change keeps to it, the one who sees both reads neither device until the code is typed; before: the owner's device took the planted item and stopped reading the member's phone); as a function over `RegistryFixture`, `memberRegistryFork` (before: the laptop registered, the phone revoked, the forged tip followed), `memberRegistrySecondGenesis` (before: no registry), `memberRegistryPinsAreSigned`. **Tracked:** the holder of the account key can keep the member's contested devices in question for as long as they like, and can make the notice return after it is settled: a denial of the member's writes in others' eyes, not a takeover. A device that anchors while only the forged branch is visible (the store withholding the genuine one, or winning the race to a device that has never seen the account) follows it until the person verifies. The cure for both is the account re-key of F10.1 (new keyset, so a new signing key in the chain), which is not built. *Mac only*: the models carry it (`DevicesModel.devicesCode`, `SharedVaultModel.verifyDevices`, and the member list's wording in `MemberRow`); no view shows the code or takes it yet. |
| Z7 | Low | The quick-unlock failure counter was written only after the prompt returned. U3 made cancels count; the count was still made in the handler that runs once the Keychain read comes back. Whoever holds the unlocked device and knows its passcode (U3's adversary) presents a face until the system prompt is about to give up, kills the app, clears the OS lockout with the passcode, and starts again: the read never returned, nothing was counted, and the three-attempt limit of F5.4 never tripped. | **Fixed.** The attempt is written to the Keychain before the prompt is shown and taken back only once the key the prompt released has opened the wrapped AUK and the keyset; a count already at the limit when the app starts refuses and deletes the Local Unlock Key without prompting. **Consequence, accepted as U8 was:** a crash or a kill during the prompt counts as a failed attempt, as does a Keychain error other than a refusal, so three of them send the person back to the password. "unlock and quick unlock" `killedDuringThePrompt` (before: still `.allowed` after three). How the system prompt behaves when its host is killed is on-device behaviour, as for U3. |
| Z8 | Medium | Found while fixing Z1; not in the audit. A change that branches off *before* the credentials a device follows (a fork) was tried at the lock screen whenever the device's own header did not open. A device that is still registered, in hands that know a password the person has since retired (one the lost device's holder enrolled before the revocation, say; the person's `.change` revokes only the device they named), publishes a fork from the old generation made with the retired password. On every device that had already followed the new password, typing the retired one, by habit or by someone who knows it standing at the device, opened it and moved it onto the fork. | **Fixed.** A fork is never tried from the lock screen: a password that does not open the device's own header is tried against pending changes only. Bringing a device over to a fork (two devices changed the password at once) is `followPasswordChange`, from a session already unlocked with the password the device follows. `forkNeedsTheCurrentPassword` (before: the retired password unlocked, and the registry followed the fork). **Left:** a puppet the lost device's holder registered before the revocation is still a registered device after it; revoking it is the person's, and the Devices screen lists it. |
| Z9 | High | Found by the review of this change (Z1, Z3). A header row ahead of the Keychain anchor was taken as this device's own interrupted step. `passwordChangePending` consulted `anchor.pending` only when the row was not ahead; when it was, it judged what was pending along whatever credential entry the row named, and `reconcileAccountObjects` then moved the anchor onto it if it descended from the pinned entry. The holder of the device the person revoked with a password change (account key, that device's key, the Secret Key, the retired password) makes a change of their own from the generation before, with the retired password under a new salt, sealing a back-dated revocation of the phone that revoked it; A8 writes its header into a lagging laptop's file as the laptop's *own* header, with the account row's columns to match, and plants its entries. The retired password opened it, nothing was pending along the planted branch, and the laptop followed it for good: the phone revoked, the revoked device active, the genuine change a fork that the new password could no longer reach from the lock screen (Z8's rule). Z1's outcome with no `passwordChangedOnAnotherDevice`, whether or not the laptop had been told of the genuine change. The same root in a crash window: a password change whose anchor write failed after the commit left the anchor on the old chain, where a fork made with the retired password counted as pending and was adopted from the lock screen. | **Fixed.** The anchor (third form, `WWRA3`, still reading `WWRA1` and `WWRA2`) carries an intent: before `adopt` or `rotateCredentials` commits, the credential entry it moves to and the SHA-256 of the exact header row it commits are written to the Keychain (`commitOwnStep`); if that write fails nothing is committed. Every decision reads `trustedAnchor`, which completes the intent when the row is that header (and the account row matches it), drops it when the account row is still at the anchor's generation, and otherwise leaves it, judged as pending. `passwordChangePending` answers *pending* for a non-empty `anchor.pending`, an unsettled intent, or a row ahead of the anchor, before any other comparison; candidates are judged along the same chain and none is tried while an intent is unsettled; reconciliation never moves an existing anchor; and sync publishes the stored header only when it names the credential the registry follows. Suite "account enrolment and device registry": `headerAheadOfTheAnchor` (told and not told; before: unlocked with the retired password and followed the planted branch, in both), `interruptedPasswordChange` (before, with the anchor left behind: the retired password adopted the fork), `interruptedAdoption`, `intentNotBorneOut`, `anchorIntentEncoding`. **Left:** an anchor left behind its row by a version that wrote no intent reads as a pending change; the device is opened with `ignoringPasswordChange` and its own change followed by name, which needs that header on the transport. |
| Z10 | Medium | Found by the review of this change (Z6, introduced by it). The forward walk over another member's registry ran up to `DeviceRegistry.maximumEntries` steps, each one to eight full replays, synchronously on the session's actor, from `refreshMemberRegistries`, which runs before any vault's log. A member (A4), or anyone a chain lists or listed, publishes a legitimate chain of thousands of password changes; at first sight, or after a lost pin, every other member's device spent minutes on it with `lock()`, AutoFill and every vault's sync, the Personal vault's included, waiting, and the pin was saved only after the walk, so a process killed meanwhile began again from the same place. | **Fixed.** One refresh follows at most `memberRegistryStepsPerRefresh` (eight) changes and the pin carries the progress to the next (`moreToFollow`); the replays run off the actor (`AwayWork.memberRegistry`), on copied inputs, and what they produced is dropped if a `lock()` came meanwhile or another refresh moved the pin. `memberRegistryWalkIsBounded` (before: one evaluation walked all twenty changes); "shared vaults through the session" `lockWinsDuringMemberRegistryReplay` (holds the replay off the actor and locks; the lock returns at once and nothing is believed). |
| Z11 | Medium | Found by the review of this change (Z6). The code that settled a member's contested devices, `memberDevicesCode`, was SHA-256 of a label, the account and a credential entry's ID, first five bytes modulo 10^12: static, unkeyed, nothing from the verifier, about 40 bits. An entry's ID covers its signature bytes, and `verifyMemberDevices` matched over everything `admit` returned (the account signature checked only), so the holder of the member's account key could grind about 10^12 hashes of junk entries for one whose code equals the genuine entry's, after which the right code was refused for good (`memberDevicesCodeUnknown`), or grind a forged change's signing nonces for a replayable change carrying the genuine code, which a verifier's device then anchored as "verified" wherever the genuine entry was withheld. ADR 0010 rejects a short static code for exactly this. | **Removed; the rest tracked.** `memberDevicesCode`, `verifyMemberDevices`, `memberDevicesCodeUnknown`, and the screen-model pieces that used them (`DevicesModel.devicesCode`, `SharedVaultModel.verifyDevices`, their wording and tests) are gone; the member list says the devices disagree and that what the contested devices write is held aside. Nothing in this version settles a contested member registry: it stays contested and is reported. Re-verifying a member's devices after a fork is to be part of the vault sharing code session of ADR 0010, whose transcript can cover the member's registry tip. Comparing safety numbers again verifies the member's account keys, which both branches share, and does not settle it ("shared vaults through the session" `revokedDeviceOfAMemberForksTheRegistry` now asserts exactly that). Meanwhile the person's way out is outside the device: what the contested devices write is held aside (not lost); the member can write from a device every branch keeps; an administrator can remove the member, who makes a new account and is invited under it. Not reproduced by grinding (2^40 work); the weakness is the design's, and its removal was decided by the coordinator of these fixes. |
| Z12 | Medium | Found by the review of this change (Z6's residual, worse than stated). A device long anchored on a member's registry moved forward whenever exactly one change followed the anchored entry, and pinned what it reached. In Z6's own scenario the laptop's holder has the keys from the moment of theft and publishes the forged branch first, with no help from the store: every member's device anchored before the theft followed it, the laptop writing as the member in every vault they share and the phone revoked; when the member's change arrived it left the chain at or before the pinned entry, so it was reported, never counted, and the takeover stood until each person verified, which Z11 could block. | **Fixed.** The pin keeps the entry first anchored (`MemberRegistryPin.base`) apart from the tip followed; a change beside any followed entry from the base on that replays is a competing branch, found earliest first: the registry stands at the entry they leave, only devices every branch keeps count, the disagreement is reported, and the tip is kept, so a branch withheld again is not taken for gone. On first sight the base is where the device lands. The saved pins gain a second format with the base (`WWMD` version 2); the first reads with the base at the tip. `firstPublishedBranchDoesNotWin` (before: the laptop still counted, the forged branch in force), `memberPinsCarryTheBase`, `moreBranchesThanReplayed`; in "shared vaults through the session" the owner that had followed the member's change forward now reads neither contested device, as the member who saw both at once does. **Consequence, accepted:** whoever holds a member's account key and a retired generation's enrolment key can now make the member contested on every device anchored before that generation, not only on devices that see both branches at once: Z6's denial, wider; never a takeover. |
| Z13 | Medium | Found by the review of this change (Z1). The way out of `tooManyPasswordChangesPending` had its own budget, spent in the adversary's order: `pendingPasswordChanges` described sixteen authors, ordered by their newest change's generation and ID, both chosen by whoever wrote it, and the Devices screen followed only a row from that list. On a device still at the old generation, the revoked device's holder, knowing the retired password and so that generation's enrolment key, registers seventeen puppets with it, each publishing a two-step chain: the lock screen's three attempts went to puppets, then `tooManyPasswordChangesPending`, and the list held sixteen puppets and not the genuine change, so the device stayed awaiting a new password for good. | **Fixed.** Every author is listed (listing needs no replay). Authors whose registration the checkpoint the device follows sealed (`sealedAuthors`: the credential entry descends from the entry that registered them) come first, in the list, in the lock screen's three attempts (`candidateHeaders`) and in the headers a sync keeps; a device registered after it may have been registered with the password it retired. What following a change would do is worked out for the first `maximumChangesDescribed` rows (`isDescribed`) and, for any other, when the person selects it (`describePasswordChange`, `DevicesModel.describe`). `puppetsDoNotCrowdOutTheGenuineChange` (before: `tooManyPasswordChangesPending`, sixteen rows, the genuine change absent); `moreAuthorsThanAttempts` now has the genuine change made on a device registered after the checkpoint, so that none is sealed and the report is still reached. **Left:** where no author is sealed (the genuine change made on a device added since the last change), the order among them is the adversary's, as before, and the person names the device. |
| Z14 | Low-Medium | Found by the review of this change (Z8). `followPasswordChange` asked only for an unlocked session, though Z8's row and its own comment said the caller "must first have unlocked it with the password it follows now": a session opened by quick unlock, or left unlocked, let Z8's adversary with a minute at it (A3) move the device onto their fork. `adopt`'s derivation ran on the actor under the candidate's parameters (its author's choice, up to the ceiling) with keys resident, so `lock()` waited behind it. And a follow that committed while another object had replaced the session's keys (a vault created, a key received) returned success with the retired Account Unlock Key resident, because it compared objects. | **Fixed.** `followPasswordChange(by:current:password:)` re-derives `current` under the stored header (`verifyAccountPassword`, off the actor) before anything else, as removing a device does; `DevicesModel.follow` asks for both. A candidate's derivation runs off the actor (`AwayWork.candidateDerivation`), at the lock screen and in a follow; a lock meanwhile zeroises what it derived and wins. The resident keys are kept if their keyset is the adopted one, whatever object holds them. `followNeedsTheCurrentPassword` and `followWithKeysReplacedMeanwhile` (before: the fork followed from a quick-unlocked session with no password; the retired key's ID still resident), `lockWinsDuringCandidateDerivation`; "Screen models: a password changed by someone else" `followTheChange` refuses a wrong current password. |
| Z15 | Low-Medium | Found by the review of this change (Z6). The pins of other members' registries are signed local state with no counter. A8 can delete the `WWMD` blob or put back an older signed copy: the device then anchors again from the transport, with the base (Z12) wherever it lands now, silently on the forged branch if only that one is visible then (A1 withholding the other). Without an adversary, each vault holds its own copy and a load takes the first in vault order. Pins are per device and not synced, so two devices of one account can hold different views (availability only). | **Tracked.** The fix keeps a digest or counter of the pins in the Keychain next to the registry anchor and refuses an older blob, saving one blob for all vaults; it changes `VaultSession+Sharing.swift`'s load and save (being reworked by the sharing group as this is written) and `SystemKeychainStore`, and needs a rule for what a mismatch does now that nothing settles a contested registry (Z11): refusing the pins re-anchors, which is what A8 wanted, and holding every member contested is a denial that a deleted file causes. Recorded as a known issue: `memberPinsDeletedFromTheFile`. The settling step that `verifyMemberDevices` interrupted vault by vault is gone (Z11). |
| Z16 | Low | Found by the review of this change; two items left open that were worse than stated. **(a)** The Z1 adversary itself (a revoked device that can create objects and delete none) can plant three well-formed headers under invented device IDs at the highest generations and the 25M-iteration ceiling, so every later enrolment spends its three derivations on them and fails; and because a header's name is `header/<generation>-<device>`, with no digest, it can create the name a device will publish its next change under before that device does: objects are never overwritten, so that header is never published, and no lagging or new device can follow the change. **(b)** Z5's "such a store publishes nothing until it has enrolled again" is false for an account from before the registry: it starts a registry at its first sync (`startRegistryIfAccountPredatesIt`), and the header it makes then seals whichever Personal vault the row names. | **(a) Tracked**: a digest in the header's name, as sharing objects have (S20), with the old names still read, is a change of the transport's naming; recorded as a known issue, `squattedHeaderName`. **(b) Documents corrected** (Z5's row, `requirePersonalVaultBinding`, architecture §6); the practical gain is small, since the row's vault must be one whose key is the account's own. |
| Z17 | Info | Found by the review of this change: claims in rows Z1 to Z8 that no test would have caught being false. | **Checked; each claim tested held**, none needing a change to the code: with `ignoringPasswordChange` a password that opens a pending candidate opens nothing; a sync keeps one candidate header per author, its newest; a follow fetches a header no sync kept (`candidateClaims`); sync's merge-and-publish gate asks the anchor, with the change gone from the file and the transport (`syncGateAsksTheAnchor`; with the `changePending` term removed the test fails); more competing changes than are replayed hold every device in question (`moreBranchesThanReplayed`); in "unlock and quick unlock", each quick unlock that opens the keyset takes its attempt back, three in a row after two cancels, and a released key that opens nothing destroys the enrolment (`attemptsTakenBackOnlyByTheKeyset`). Held by Z9 to Z14's tests: more than sixteen authors, a lock during `followPasswordChange`, the `keysetVersion > anchor.generation` branch, a long walk, a branch published first to an anchored device. Not tested: a bound on the listing loop's verifications (Z2), Z5's hard failure in `reconcileAccountObjects` (unreachable once the unlock has checked the same) and `openVaultKeys`' vault check on its own. |

What this part of the audit did not settle. **Decisions that are the owner's.** Whether the lock screen should offer
the choice between pending password changes itself (it is offered from an unlocked session only, by a screen model
no view shows yet); how a member's contested devices are re-verified within ADR 0010's vault sharing code session
(the member devices code is gone, Z11, and until that session exists nothing settles a contested registry); what a
Keychain digest of the member pins should do on a mismatch (Z15); the header naming change of Z16; and the account
re-key (F10.1), which is what ends Z6's and Z12's residual and the reading a revoked device keeps (D9b). **The
Mac.** Everything here was built and run on Linux against swift-crypto. Nothing under `Apps/` was changed;
`SystemKeychainStore` stores the anchor (now its third form, Z9) as opaque bytes and `SQLiteSharingStore` the
`memberDevices` value (now its second format, Z12) under a key it has not seen, both unrun there. The screen models
changed by Z11, Z13 and Z14 (`DevicesModel`, `SharedVaultModel`, `MemberRow`) are portable and tested; no view uses
them yet.

### The sync engine and its local state (Z20 to Z39)

The adversaries are those of architecture §2: **A1** the sync store, **A4** a current member of a shared vault whose
role may write, **A5** a former member, **A8** whoever edits this device's files while the app is locked, and an
insider device of the account. Each row was reproduced by a test that failed before its fix, unless it says otherwise;
"Linux only" marks what was run with swift-crypto and the in-memory stores, and *Mac only* what compiles only with
SQLite and has not run yet. Z39 is the last number of this block: the rows from the review of the second round of these
fixes (Z30 to Z39) continue at Z135, after the sharing and files blocks' rows, because this one was full, and those from
the review of the third round (Z135 to Z143) at Z144, and those from the review of the fourth (Z144 to Z149) at
Z150.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Z20 | High | The sync state row was believed as read, and what it said went out signed. N14 signed the stash and the review of milestone 3 the pending deletions so that "a row planted in the file erases nothing", but `SyncState` itself passed through `AuthenticatedSyncState` unchecked: `erasures` became the tombstones of this device's next snapshot, `merged` its coverage vector and every segment's acknowledgements, and a reconciling sync, the documented repair, kept the erasures. A8, holding no key, deletes an item's row, plants an erasure for it (a summary naming every device ID in the plaintext columns, a stamp just above the row) and a made-up stream at 300 so that a snapshot is due; the device seals and signs the tombstone, every other device erases the item, and no replay brings it back. Or A8 sets `merged` for another device to 2^48: the signed coverage moves that device's next sequence number to 2^48 + 1 (Z21), where no reader lists a segment, for good. N19 and S13 recorded the state as unauthenticated with "advisory kinds only" as the consequence; that was wrong. | **Fixed.** The session signs the whole row (`SyncState.authenticator`, over `"WWSS" ‖ vault ID ‖ device ID ‖` every field in one fixed encoding, `SyncState.authenticatedBytes`) with the account key under a new `SignatureDomain.syncState` (raw value 10), and verifies it on every load, with the Keychain's copy of the key while locked. A row that does not verify is no state: the engine starts from nothing and replays the log, which rebuilds the erasures from signed objects. The session's own readers of the row (`takeWriteCut`, whose result is signed, and the rows carried to a new device ID) read the verified one. A state cannot be saved while locked; the sync then reports `deferredUntilUnlock` and the row saved after the last merged segment stands. Signatures stop planting, not rollback: an older row this device signed is still its own, and costs a replay of what it had merged since. Tests: `plantedErasure`, `plantedMergedVector`, `stateRowOfAnotherVault` ("sync with real keys"; all failed before), `SyncStateAuthenticationTests` (every field is covered; a count trips when one is added). |
| Z21 | High | A member's snapshot in a shared vault was merged with its coverage vector and tombstones unjudged. A shared vault writes no snapshots (`maintainsLog` is off), but `sharedVerify` accepted one from any account that may write, and the engine believed its vector for every stream whose next segment it did not hold, the reader's own included: A4 publishes an empty snapshot naming every other device at 2^48, each reader stops fetching those streams and takes 2^48 + 1 as its own next sequence number, `push` writes there and reports success, every reader ignores the name, and a replay keeps the number, so the device stays mute in that vault after A4 is removed. The same snapshot's tombstones reached the store without `admitsErasure` (S45, S46), contradicting §8.10. An own-account insider reaches the coverage half in any vault. | **Fixed, and not as first proposed.** Refusing every snapshot in a vault with a judge would have stopped each stream whose first segments were collected before the vault was shared, on a new member's device and on every device at its next replay (`snapshotsBridgeWhatWasCollectedBeforeSharing` holds that case), and would have put back items deleted before sharing whose tombstones only a snapshot still carries. So: (a) a snapshot of a shared vault must be signed by the **owner's** account, whose devices were the vault's only writers before it was shared (`sharedVerify`); (b) a snapshot's tombstones are judged like a segment's (`WriteJudge.admitsErasure`), and one not admitted is held aside; (c) in every vault, a snapshot's coverage of **this device's own stream** counts no further than the stream can have got (what the device remembers publishing or merging, and its own objects on the transport that verify, Z24); (d) `push` never writes at a number beyond `maximumSequence` and reports `ownStreamExhausted`, marking nothing as sent. Tests: `memberSnapshotCoverage`, `memberSnapshotTombstone`, `ownerSnapshotTombstoneIsJudged` ("shared vaults through the session"), `coverageOfOwnStream`, `exhaustedStream` ("sync log"), `snapshotTombstonesAreJudged`; all failed before. **Accepted:** the owner's account (and in a vault of one account, any of its devices) can still sign coverage of *another* device's stream that the reader believes, and so make readers pass over segments of it; that signer can delete the vault's contents outright. |
| Z22 | Medium | Record identity in the local store is (record ID, kind) across all vaults, and a collision rejected the whole object. A4, a writer in one shared vault, publishes a record there under an ID that will arrive in another of the victim's vaults (an item of a second shared vault, a scan record under an item's ID, or the derivable ID of an item moved out). On a device that stores the plant first, the honest record's segment was rejected with `recordInAnotherVault`; a stream is merged strictly in sequence, so its author's stream stopped there at every sync, and a snapshot was refused whole. N5 had recorded the collision for scan records as inert. | **Fixed in the engine; the store's key is tracked.** A collision costs the colliding entry alone: it is kept in the quarantine (`SyncReport.collided`, shown as `QuarantineReason.recordInAnotherVault`), the other vault's row is not touched, and the object and its stream are merged. Tests: `collisionCostsOneEntry`, `collisionInSnapshot`, `crossVaultRecordID` ("sync log"; the first two failed before). **Tracked:** keying rows by (vault, record, kind) in `RecordStore` and `SQLiteRecordStore`, which removes the collision; it is a schema migration of the App Group database that cannot be built or run here, and every `store.record(RecordKey)` caller changes with it. Until then the kept entry is merged only by a replay after the other vault's row is gone, and a move's derived ID stays predictable to members of the source vault. Not written: the session-level run with a real judge that the audit asked for. |
| Z23 | Low | Five places wrote "one above the row read" with a plain `+ 1` on a version the file controls: the account header's row (a password change, and adopting one at unlock), a vault's directory row (deleting the vault, and a deletion fetched from another device), and `VaultMetadataMerger` over a local row that does not open. A8 sets such a row to `UInt64.max` (nothing authenticated binds it) and the process traps: at every sync, or at every attempt to change the password. Y11 and Y24 had fixed the cases an insider reaches. | **Fixed.** `VaultSession.version(replacing:)` throws `VaultError.versionOverflow`; the merger throws `ItemMergeError.versionOverflow`, so the object is refused and reported and other vaults sync; a fetched deletion leaves such a row as it is. Tests: `headerRowAtTheLastVersion`, `directoryRowAtTheLastVersion`, `fetchedDeletionOverTheLastVersion` ("account enrolment and device registry"), `metadataRowAtTheLastVersion` ("sync with real keys"); each trapped before. A8 can still deny service more simply (the account row), so this is hardening. |
| Z24 | Medium | One unauthenticated object name in a device's own stream broke or muted that device's publishing. Y8's fix took the next sequence number from the transport's listing, which is names alone, read before any fetch or signature. A1, or anyone who can create a file under the vault's prefix (a read-only or former member of a shared folder), plants any bytes at `log/<victim>/<2^48>`: the victim's next segment goes to 2^48 + 1, which every reader ignores, its rows are marked as sent, and the saved number outlives the file. Planted a little past the head it left a hole no device could fill. Reported while the object was listed (`rejected`, `waiting`), silent afterwards; in an own vault the victim's rows still travelled in its snapshots, in a shared vault nothing carried them. | **Fixed as far as a name goes; the squatted number is tracked.** The number comes only from own-stream objects that were fetched and carry this device's signatures (checked again for ones staged earlier). An object shown not to be this device's (not a signed object, wrong signatures, over the size cap) moves nothing and is reported as before; one that cannot be told (listed and not there, unreadable, no key to check with) holds publishing back for that sync (`SyncReport.ownStreamUnverified`), so a restored device neither adopts a name nor publishes below its own head, which is what Y8 was for. Tests: `junkFarAheadInOwnStream`, `junkJustAheadInOwnStream`, `unreadableOwnSegment` ("sync log"; failed before). **Tracked:** an object squatting at exactly the victim's next number still stops that vault's publishing while it is there, reported at every sync (`ownStreamConflict`, and held-back deletions in the other vaults), with nothing marked as sent and no state changed, and it ends when the object is removed. Passing it needs a skip the author signs and readers honour, which changes "never skip a gap" and a shared vault's cut, a chain over consecutive segments; for the store (A1) this is the withholding Y7 accepts. |
| Z25 | Medium | The version-jump ceiling (U5) was measured against whatever the reading device held at the moment it met a version. Versions are one clock per vault that every save, merge, re-seal and weekly check advances. A device of the account publishes one version exactly 2^20 above the vault's highest and writes again the row that held that highest, so no stored row lies below the jump; every current device accepted each step. A device that joins later starts from a snapshot holding nothing, whose entries come in record-ID order, and quarantined every row above the gap; nothing merged a quarantined entry afterwards, and its own low versions were accepted by everyone, so the split was one-way and showed only as entries in the held-aside list. About a million honest writes reach the same state. A replayed log has the same trouble with the order it is read in: one stream after another, a segment in record-ID order. In a shared vault the log is whole and a second replay healed it. | **Fixed.** (a) Whatever a merge pass refuses as out of reach is tried again, lowest version first, once the pass has merged everything else (`retryOutOfReach`); the entry stays in the quarantine in between, so a lock loses nothing, and only what is still out of reach remains there. (b) A device that holds nothing of a vault, in a vault with no judge, takes the snapshots it starts from as its baseline: their entries are held to `maximumVersion` alone. Each is the state of a device of the account that accepted every version in it. Tests: `lateJoinerAcrossAJump`, `jumpThatIsOnlyAnOrderOfReading` ("sync log"; failed before), `versionJumps` unchanged. **Accepted:** a device of the account can thereby hand a new device a clock of its choosing below 2^62, and that device's own writes would then be quarantined by the others; a device with any history keeps the ceiling for snapshots too. A join that takes several syncs across such a gap (a second snapshot not yet visible at the first) is still cut off; nothing short of deleting the device's copy of the vault repairs that, and it is not tracked further. |
| Z26 | Low | A device that kept a version aside (a U5 jump, Y37's join that does not fit in one record, now Z22's collision) counted the segment that carried it as merged and went on to write ordinary snapshots whose coverage included that segment and whose entries did not include the version. Once every stream had acknowledged and the author collected the segment, a device joining later was never shown the version: `docs/10-sync-correctness.md` §8 rests collection on every ordinary snapshot being the state of a device that merged everything it covers, argued for unknown kinds only. No adversary is needed; the version survives on its author and in other devices' quarantines, so the loss also needs the author to disappear. | **Fixed.** While its quarantine holds a version written by a device still trusted, a device writes no ordinary snapshot (`SyncReport.snapshotWithheld`, `snapshotWithheldForQuarantine`), only the one it owes a revoked device's work, exactly as ADR 0009 has it for entries it cannot read; the others step in at four times the threshold, and it writes again once the person has discarded what was kept. Test: `quarantineWithholdsSnapshot` ("sync log"; failed before). **Accepted:** what the person discards on one device is gone from that device's later snapshots, as any discard is; and an insider can hold a household's snapshots back by having every device quarantine something of its own, for as long as it is trusted and nobody looks at the list (as N17 for the stash). |
| Z27 | Medium | The quarantine was unbounded and each insert reread all of it. `sharedVerify` accepts every segment of an account whose permission ended, at any position; the judge answers "hold aside" for one beyond the cut, and every entry of it was kept, "until the person discards them", with no cap on entries (256 a segment) or bytes (2 MiB an entry). `SQLiteSyncStateStore.quarantine` found a duplicate by loading the vault's whole quarantine, envelopes included, per insert, and a shared vault replays its log at every change of head (S50), offering them all again. A5 who can still create objects (a file share, lag in removal, or with A1), or A4 through implausible versions, fills the disk of every member's device and makes each sync quadratic in what is kept. S50 had recorded forced replays as linear. | **Fixed in the engine and the in-memory store; the SQLite half is *Mac only* and unproven until a Mac run passes.** The engine holds a vault's quarantine to `maximumQuarantinedEntries` and `maximumQuarantinedBytes` (2,048 and 64 MiB), and to a share of them for the versions any one device wrote (512 and 16 MiB); past a cap a version is not kept on this device and `SyncReport.quarantineFull` counts it, while the object that carried it is merged or passed as before (a shared vault's log, never collected, still holds it). An entry already kept is found by lookup: a set in memory, and in SQLite an indexed 64-bit digest (FNV-1a, not trusted: SQLite compares the columns of whatever matches) in a new table at schema version 3, to which the earlier table's rows are carried, with a stored size so that usage is counted without reading an envelope. Tests: `quarantineIsCapped` ("sync log"), `heldAsideSegmentsAreCapped`, `quarantineLookupAndUsage` (both stores; SQLite and `migratesFromVersion1` on a Mac). The first two failed before. **Tracked:** refusing at download a segment an ended account's cut cannot admit needs the cut where the engine downloads without keys, which is a change to `SyncKeyring` and `sharedVerify`, not to the engine alone; until then such a segment costs a fetch and an open at each replay. The versions an entry's `device` field names are its writer's claim: an account that registers many devices gets a share for each, and the vault's cap is what holds. The session's own take-out of local versions is not capped. |
| Z28 | Low | A deletion was committed before its pending tombstone was signed and recorded. `DeletionRecordingStore.commit` committed the change and then asked the session for one signature per deleted row; the session signs only while unlocked, so a lock that landed after the commit (auto-lock, the app going to the background) left rows deleted on this device with nothing to publish. Deletions are never inferred from a missing row, so the items stayed everywhere else; a vault deleted that way was deleted on every device while its remaining items stayed on all the others as what a deletion had not seen. A crash in the same window does the same. | **Fixed for the lock; the crash is accepted.** The tombstones are made, and signed, before the commit (`SyncStateStore.prepared`) and recorded after it, which needs no key: a lock while they are being made fails the deletion before anything has changed, and one after the commit changes nothing. Nothing is recorded for a deletion whose commit fails. Tests: `lockAfterTheCommit` (failed before), `lockBeforeTheCommit` ("Vault session: a lock during a deletion"). **Accepted,** as `DeletionRecordingStore` has always said and architecture §6 repeats: the two stores are separate files, so a crash between the commit and the record loses the tombstone, and the record comes back from another device at a reconciling sync or its next edit there, rather than a deletion that never happened being published. An intent row in the record store's own transaction would close it and needs the SQLite store. |
| Z29 | Low | An entry naming this device was recorded as *published* whatever became of it. Whoever can put a signed object in a device's own stream, or sign a snapshot that attributes a version to it (in a shared vault a member who lists the victim's device ID in their own registry, S33; in any vault a device of the account), plants an older state of an item sealed again at a higher version. The victim's copy, holding an edit not yet published, contains everything the plant says, so the merge keeps it; but the plant's stamp was now above the row in `SyncState.published`, the outbox passed the row over, and the edit stayed on that device until the item was edited again. The audit left one step unverified; the reproduction confirmed it. | **Fixed in the engine.** A version found in the log under this device's name counts as published only if it is the row this device holds (version, device, key and bytes), or its own deletion; a row that differs is published again, which costs a segment and merges to nothing. Test: `plantedOwnVersionDoesNotSuppressPublishing` ("sync log"; failed before). Writing into another member's device stream at all is the sharing group's finding. |
| Z30 | High | Found by the review of this change (Z24, Z21). In a shared vault an object at a location in this device's own stream verifies whenever an account the chain lists has a device registered under that device ID (S33), and the engine took "verifies in my stream" for "mine". A4 (or A5, whose segments still verify) registers a device under the victim's ID and signs one segment at the victim's head + 5: the victim's next number went past it, it published there and marked its rows as sent, and every reader waited at head + 1 for good, with no snapshot in a shared vault to bridge it. At 2^48 the same object exhausted the stream, which a replay kept and the member's removal did not end. The engine publishes nothing there (`sharedEngineOptions(ownIDCommits:)`), reported (`SharedVaultSyncReport.deviceIDDoesNotCommit`); `syncVerifyOwn` leaves such a device's own objects there undecided; `holdsUnpublishedWork` counts, in a shared vault, every row of such a device holding an edit of its own, whatever its state says, so a copy an earlier build published there holds every vault's deletions back. `approveDevice` and `enrol(as:)` refuse an identity whose ID does not commit (`VaultError.deviceIDDoesNotCommit`); the package `enrol(assigningDeviceID:)` does for a prepared key, and keeps a test's named ID otherwise (no production path names one). Tests: `moveByANonCommittingDevice` (failed with the gate's rule disabled: the original was deleted on the account's other device and the copy reached nobody), `nonCommittingDeviceTakesNoPart`, `legacyDeviceDoesNotShare` ("shared vaults through the session"), `approvalRefusesNonCommittingIdentity`, `enrolmentRefusesNonCommittingIdentity` ("account enrolment and device registry"). Linux only. Test sessions playing an adversary's device under another's ID are modified clients (`playModifiedClient`, package only). **Owner decision (taken, the safest):** a device of an account from before IDs committed takes no part in shared vaults (no share, accept, write or publish) until it is enrolled again as a new device, whose ID commits; it reads and writes its private vaults as before. There is no in-app re-enrolment yet: today that is erasing the app's data on the device once its private work is published, and enrolling it again (a restore onto other hardware registers as a new device already). Keeping the old ID usable would need every member to accept a listing that commits to nothing, which is S33 again. **Tracked:** a vault holding values stamped by such a device, shared later from a device whose ID commits, has those values held aside on every device once a member is listed (in the quarantine, nothing lost); `shareVault` does not look for them. A device that published into a shared vault under an earlier build holds every vault's deletions back until it is enrolled again. |

Test: `ownSegmentsBelowTheFloorMergeWhenListed` ("sync log"; failed before: `merged[self]` was 6 and items 3 to 5 never came back). `restoredStateAboveItsOwnAccountsWitness` (3 to 5 gone for good) still passes. |

What this part of the audit did not settle. **The Mac.** `SQLiteSyncStateStore`'s schema version 3 (Z27), the
`authenticator` field of its state row (Z20), and the review's changes to it (the paged migration, clamped sizes, capped
digest rows, Z39) were written on Linux, where the file does not compile; its contract tests, the migration test and the
session over real files have not run, nor has `SQLiteConnection.Failure`'s conformance to `StorageFailure` in
`SQLiteRecordStore.swift`. **The store's key.** Z22's root, one key space for every vault's records, stays until
`SQLiteRecordStore` is keyed by vault. **A squatted number.** Z24 leaves an unsigned object at exactly a device's next
sequence number able to stop that device's publishing in one vault while it is there (since Z157 so does another
member's signed object, which verifies nowhere; Z30 and Z137 had stepped over it), and a device that has never published in a vault can be held there by an unfetchable
name (Z31). **Snapshots and trust.** Z21 and Z25 leave whoever may sign a snapshot (a device of the account; in a shared
vault its owner's) believed about other devices' coverage and, by a device with nothing of the vault, about versions:
the engine cannot bound either without the listing or a history, and both signers can do more directly. **The
quarantine's rows.** They are not signed and a kept entry is never taken out when a version that supersedes it is merged
(Z37), and a snapshot's entries are charged to the device each names (Z38): each wants a column of the SQLite schema. A
snapshot by an insider can also hold a device with no anchor back from publishing, reported, while it stands (Z32).
**Not written.** The session-level tests the audit asked for Z22 (a real judge, two vaults) and Z27 (cost measured on
SQLite). **After the review of the second round (Z135 to Z143).** A read-only member's object in another member's
stream is now stepped over (Z137); junk, or an object under substituted keys or by an account the chain does not list,
still squats where Z24 tracks, and is worded. The anchor survives a planted name (Z135) but not a store that withholds a
device's head while re-listing an older object of its own, which wants the collected floor in the signed state; a
snapshot's claim holds a device back only while its stream lists something there (Z139), and a claim an honest device
carried on is not traced to its signer. The quarantine's digest is still FNV-1a: a crowded digest is now a counted drop
(Z141), and a keyed digest is a schema migration. **After the review of the third round (Z144 to Z149).** Nothing of
a revoked device's, or of the owner's streams collected before sharing, is waited for (Z144), but what only a revoked
device's own versions carried does not reach a device that joins a shared vault later. The hold for a device's own
stream now rests on its signed state and trusted claims, never on a name (Z145), at the price of a lost state whose head
is unusable; a read-only member names no stream (Z146), while a writer still can, reported; a crowded digest costs one
replay and withholds snapshots until the person discards entries, an owner decision (Z147). **After the review of the fourth round (Z150 to
Z156).** A revocation counts only from the account the device is (Z150), but a member who lists another account's
revoked device as registered still keeps its work held aside on devices that never read its stream. A stream is named by
a segment whose signer could write under its key (Z152), which costs a rotation after each promotion, an owner decision;
the owner's streams are taken for collected only where a snapshot shows it (Z151); and the judged basis binds the
revocations a judgement rested on (Z153). **After the review of the fifth round (Z157 to Z161).** Whose a stream is
was inferred for six rounds, and each inference was attacked; device IDs now commit to their account (Z157), so a
stream, a stamp and a revocation each name one account by identity, and Z46, Z146, Z150, Z152 and Z155 are superseded
with their residuals (the rotation after a promotion among them). What it costs: another member's signed object at a
device's next number squats like junk (Z24's tracked class) instead of being stepped over, an owner decision; and
what a revoked device of an account that may no longer write published is still missing from an index built after
the revocation (Z158). A device restored to an older state publishes above what its own account's devices merged
(Z159). **After the review of the sixth round (Z162 to Z166).** A device whose ID does not commit takes no part in a
shared vault and nothing it published there counts as published (Z162); a device of an account from before IDs
committed must be enrolled again as a new device, an owner decision, and values such a device stamped are held aside
once its vault is shared. A device publishing above a witnessed floor merges its own earlier segments when they appear
(Z163). A member's object at a device's next number now names its signer, and still stops that stream and the device's
deletions everywhere until removed; the signed skip stays open (Z164). An ID two committed registries list counts in
neither (Z165). A removed member's flood into their own streams still grows the index (Z166). **After the review of
the seventh round (Z167 to Z169).** A restore onto other hardware parks the old ID's deletions while a shared vault
holds an edit of it nobody received, and the in-app re-enrolment to come must do the same (Z167); the package's test
hooks are held to tests by a tripwire (Z168); a device key that cannot be read is a retry, not a reason to enrol again
(Z169). **After the review of the eighth round (Z170 to Z172).** One predicate decides parking, its release and the
deletion gate, so a second restore releases nothing and a deletion made while parked waits too, and the hold covers
every vault while a shared vault holds such a copy; where this device may neither move nor delete it, nothing
releases the deletions, an owner decision (Z170). The summary names the copies and the way out (Z171), and the
tripwire matches lines, not files, and every spelling of the hooks (Z172). **After the review of the ninth round (Z173
to Z178).** The predicate reads the values a row holds, never its clock's claims, so another account cannot hold this
account's deletions back (Z173); a registry lists a non-committing ID in production only for an account from before the
registry or through the account's own modified client, which bounds Z170 and Z173. The report is made only while
unlocked and survives an unreadable directory (Z174, Z175), its summary is cleared by a lock (Z176), registration fails
rather than strand parked deletions (Z177), and the tripwire counts allowed lines (Z178). Still tracked from that review:
computing the scan once per sync rather than in each gate and the report (I4, left: the gate must see each pass's
rows), and I5, kept as the reviewer recorded it. Every one of these is Linux only, like the rest of the session's sharing.

### Shared vaults: verification, membership and the session's sharing paths (Z40 to Z59)

The adversaries are those of architecture §2: the sync store and key directory (**A1**), a current member (**A4**), a
former member (**A5**) and someone with the device's files (**A8**). Each finding was reproduced by a test that failed
on the code as audited unless its row says otherwise; *Mac only* marks what was changed under `Apps/` and has not been
compiled. Z59 is the last number of this block: the rows from the second review of these fixes continue at Z120,
after the CI block, because this one was full.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Z40 | Critical | "Compared by eye" pinned whatever the key directory returned on a second lookup. `contactCandidate` fetched the bundle whose safety number the screen showed and dropped it; `verifyContact` and `acceptInvitation` fetched again, and `VerificationEvidence.comparedByEye` carried nothing to check the second answer against. Whoever serves the directory (A1; with a file-based directory any writer of the shared folder, so A4) showed the real number, then answered with a bundle of its own: on the inviting side the next invitation wrapped the vault key to it; on the accepting side the chain was anchored on it, so a chain it signed, listing the invitee's real keys with a grant of a key it knew, was joined and everything saved there was its to read. The toggle is offered on both screens and is what the UI tests use. Q2 had fixed only the pasted code. | **Fixed.** Every kind of evidence names the keys it is about: `.comparedByEye(shown:)` carries the fingerprint the screen displayed, and `VerifiedContacts.verify` refuses (`fingerprintMismatch`) a bundle with any other, as it already did for a typed number or a scanned code; the screens pass the fingerprint of the step that was shown (`FingerprintConfirmation.evidence(showing:)`). Not a defect in itself; each was a place a defect could sit unseen. | **Checked; each claim tested held.** Tests added, none of which needed a change to the code: both sides of an invitation refusing `comparedByEye(shown:)` for a bundle other than the one shown (Z40, "sharing and membership"); every other write path of the session under `ledgerUnavailable` (trash, restore, permanent deletion, the purge, a move out and in, a copy in, a login saved and updated through the AutoFill port, a conflict resolved, an import preview, `removeLocalCopies`), with nothing changed, queued or published (Z41, Z44, Z45; `AdversarialSharedVaultClosedWritePathTests.swift`); a marker outliving the revocation of the device that wrote it, and a device enrolled afterwards holding the vault closed before its first sync (Z41); the sync-state half of the whole-log check, with the snapshot no longer listed (Z42); the deletion gate's first clause, a copy written in a vault that is then held closed (Z44; fails with the clause removed); a key no record held here introduced not being passed over (Z47). Z43's and Z46's are in rows Z51 and Z53. **Not reached:** the `!markersLoaded` answer and `ensureSharedMarkers` writing a marker `markShared` could not (Z41); the synced scan record's write under `ledgerUnavailable` and an import committed after the ledger is lost in the same session; the purge and a permanent deletion while a removal is owed or the chain has gone on in a later format (Z44); `removeLocalCopies` removing scan results (Z45); the `writersOwn` exemption and the refusal of a snapshot named as this device's (Z46); a losing branch that comes to win, whose key then replays the log (Z47). |
| Z59 | Medium | Found by the second review of these fixes (Z48's "Left", worse than stated). A member's new device that holds nothing of a vault had nothing to compare an invitation's owner with.
