Whoever can serve content for `download.swift.org` to the VM (T3, or the session's intercepting egress proxy) had only to fail the check to reach the path with none. swiftly was also downloaded on every run. | **Fixed.** A tarball that arrives and cannot be shown to be the pinned key's ends the attempt; the session is told it may be tampering and to install from nowhere else unasked. swiftly is fetched and run only when the tarball could not be downloaded at all and `WATCHWORD_ALLOW_UNVERIFIED_SWIFTLY=1` is set (`09-cloud-sessions.md` §3). `test_cloud_setup.py` runs the script itself against stand-ins in a scratch root: "a failed verification stops and tries nothing else", "an unavailable tarball does not reach swiftly unasked"; both failed before. **Tracked:** swiftly is not pinned, because no published checksum for it was at hand to check one against; the script has not run on a cloud VM since the change. |
| Z107 | Medium | Architecture §2 listed "Secure Enclave wrapping" among A2's primary mitigations and §4 said the Secret Key is "additionally wrapped by a Secure Enclave P-256 key where available"; the plan assigned the wrap to milestone 2, recorded as done. It was never built: `SystemKeychainStore` stores the Secret Key as a generic-password item of class `WhenPasscodeSetThisDeviceOnly` and nothing more, and nothing in `Sources/` or `Apps/` creates an Enclave key. A thief with a locked device who can extract a Keychain item of that class (A2 with the passcode, or a forensic extraction) holds the Secret Key itself, where the documents led a reader to expect a second hardware-bound layer. | **Documents corrected; the wrap is tracked** with W11. §2, §4 and the plan say what is built and what the gap is worth: an extraction of that class already defeats the Keychain's own hardware binding, and an Enclave key without access control is usable by anything that runs as the app. Building it needs an amendment to ADR 0004 and an adversarial test. No code changed. |
| Z108 | Low | Architecture §13.1 said the breach-check service learns five characters of a password's SHA-1 per check and the times, "and nothing else". No request carries more. But one device asks about every password from one address each week, in a repeating order and with one item's fields back to back, so across requests the service holds a set of prefixes that identifies the vault whatever its address, the set's size, its changes (a changed password, with the day), and which prefixes belong to one item; with breach data that names people it can test the set against a known person's leaked passwords. | **Documents corrected.** §13.1 states what is learnt per request and across requests, that the full set each week is the upper bound (a household's synced checks defer a device's own for up to 28 days), and that the identification estimate follows from the prefix length and is not measured. `CLAUDE.md`'s first hygiene invariant keeps its per-request statement and forbids describing the checks as telling the service nothing. Shuffling the weekly order and splitting an item's fields across steps would remove the order and the grouping but not the set; not built, for the owner. |

Found by the review of this change (2026-11-17): an independent reviewer set out to break the checks above, ran what
could be run on Linux, and found these. Each was reproduced by a script test that failed first.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Z109 | High | The new action audit passed an unpinned local action. Its pattern for `owner/repo@<sha>` let the owner be `.`, so `uses: ./ci/build@<40 hex>` read as pinned; to GitHub anything that starts `./` is a path in the checkout, the `@…` included, and the composite action's manifest there, the pull request's own file and outside `.github/`, was never read. A pull request (T5) could so run any action by tag through a manifest the audit did not open, with `Required checks` green. | **Fixed.** The owner must be an account name (it starts with a letter or digit), no path segment may be `.` or `..`, and every value that starts with `.` is refused as a path in the checkout: no local action is permitted at all, which costs nothing, since the repository has none (`07-supply-chain.md` §3.1). `test_audit_actions.py` "a local path is not pinned by ending like a commit". |
| Z110 | Medium | The adversarial-suite check tested the bytes before `Suite "…" passed` with `[[:alnum:]]` in the caller's locale. A byte-oriented awk in a Latin-1 locale classes the first byte of the tick (or of an SF Symbols glyph) as a letter, so every genuine pass was reported missing: a gate that fails on a developer's or a runner's locale teaches people to distrust it. Reproduced with mawk under a generated `en_US.ISO-8859-1`. | **Fixed.** Every awk in the check runs with `LC_ALL=C` and reads the log as bytes. `test_audit_tasks.py` "the result symbol is not a letter in any locale" (generates the locale where `localedef` exists; skips otherwise) and "awk is run in the C locale whatever the caller's". |
| Z111 | Medium | The "manifest is one value everywhere" check of Z104 was a short list of words and several ways round it were not on it: `private import Foundation` and the other access-modified imports; an import on a line that a filter read as a comment (`/*`, then `// */ import Foundation`) or after a statement on the same line; and, with no import, `MemoryLayout<CLongDouble>.size`, `randomElement()`, `shuffled()`, `hashValue`, `ContinuousClock.now`, `NSHomeDirectory()` and `FileManager`. | **Fixed as far as a list can be; the rest tracked.** No block comment may appear in a manifest; the word `import` on any line that is not a comment must be the whole line `import PackageDescription`; and the names above are refused. `test_dependency_tripwire.py` "a manifest that reads its environment is refused" (each case), "the one import is the whole line". **Tracked:** a list of words cannot be complete (the iteration order of a `Set` needs no name at all). The check that would not depend on one is to dump the manifest on Linux and on macOS, under more than one environment, and compare the results; not built. Until then the control is the review of `Package.swift` (`07-supply-chain.md` §2.1), and the script says so. |
| Z112 | Low | A pass was believed beside its own contradiction: a suite reported `skipped`, with a line elsewhere that reads as its pass (a test title with a line break in it, or a printed line), still counted as run. And a suite whose every `@Test` is individually disabled is reported by Swift Testing as passed (from its behaviour; not run here). | **Fixed, with a stated limit.** An area fails if any line reports its suite skipped or failed, or if the words of its pass appear on more than one line; and `test:adversarial` fails on any `Test … skipped` line. `test_audit_tasks.py` "a pass beside a skip or a failure is not a pass", "a suite reported passed twice is not trusted", "a skipped test fails the adversarial run". **Accepted:** a line printed by a test, when it is the only one, cannot be told from Swift Testing's own. Whoever can add a test can forge its log; that is inherent in reading a log, and review of the tests answers it. The form of the skipped-test line is from Swift Testing's source, not from a run. |
| Z113 | Low | The spec allowlist accepted any `product:` name of the `Watchword` package reference, `Crypto` and `CryptoExtras` among them, so an app target could link BoringSSL (which ADR 0006 confines to Linux) by naming swift-crypto's product through the package graph. Whether XcodeGen and Xcode resolve a transitive product that way was not run. | **Fixed.** A product must be `SecureMemory` or a name starting `Watchword`, and never `WatchwordTestSupport`. `test_dependency_tripwire.py` "prebuilt and foreign dependencies are refused". |
| Z114 | Low | Z106's opt-in was documented as a variable set on the cloud environment, and the failure note told the session the same. A setup script is not given the environment's saved variables, so the documented switch did nothing: a person who chose the fallback would not have got it. | **Documents and note corrected.** The opt-in is a line above the pasted script, `export WATCHWORD_ALLOW_UNVERIFIED_SWIFTLY=1`, or a prefix on a run by hand; `09-cloud-sessions.md` §2 and §3 and the note say so. `test_cloud_setup.py` "the opt-in is a line above the pasted script", and the note's wording in "an unavailable tarball does not reach swiftly unasked". |
| Z115 | Low | `cloud-setup.sh` verified who signed the tarball, not which release it was. The pinned key has signed every Swift release, so whoever serves the URL (T3) could answer a request for the pinned version with an older toolchain and its genuine signature, known faults included. | **Fixed.** Once extracted, the toolchain must report the release that was asked for (`swift --version` naming the tag); one that does not is removed and treated as one that did not verify, so nothing else is tried. `test_cloud_setup.py` "another release signed by the same key is not the one asked for". |
| Z116 | Low | The YAML reader accepted one construct a full parser rejects: a block scalar whose first lines are whitespace longer than its indentation. Not a different reading, but against "refuses everything outside the subset", on which the reader's claim rests. | **Fixed.** A whitespace-only line longer than its block scalar's indentation is refused, before the first line of text (where a parser rejects it) and after (where the extra spaces are content the reader would drop). `test_yaml_reader.py` "everything outside the subset is refused" (three cases), "blank lines in a block scalar are still read". |
| Z117 | Low | Z105's inspection had no positive control: "none of the markers" is also what a search reports of a binary it cannot read, or of a build that produced the wrong thing. | **Fixed; *Mac only*.** The app must contain `Watchword cannot reach its shared storage`, a string literal on the path every launch takes, over 15 bytes and so stored as bytes, found by the same search in the app outside its embedded extension; without it the inspection fails. The control must still be in the sources, outside `#if DEBUG`. `test_app_tasks.py` "a binary the search cannot read proves nothing", "the control is a literal the app always compiles". One literal shows the search can read that binary; it does not show that every kind of literal is stored alike. |
| Z118 | Low | With `Apps/project.yml` missing, the allowlist was skipped and the script printed its all-clear, so deleting or renaming the spec was a way past Z103. | **Fixed.** `Apps/` without `Apps/project.yml` fails. `test_dependency_tripwire.py` "a missing spec is a failure, not a pass". |

Found from a run (2026-11-18), not by the audit: no auditor or reviewer read the gate this way, and nothing in the
tree could have shown it. A real run on `71eb8c9` went green with a required leg that never ran.

| ID | Severity | Finding and threat | Disposition |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Z119 | Medium | `Required checks`, the one check branch protection names, judged `needs.*.result`, and `ci.yml` said a matrix job's result is its legs' together, so that needing the job needs every leg. That is false for a leg cancelled before it ran. In run 44175108735 (attempt 1, on `71eb8c9`) the leg `Package tests (Xcode 16.4 / macOS 15)` waited 33 minutes for a runner and was cancelled by the platform: no runner name, no steps, conclusion `cancelled`. Every other job succeeded, `needs.package-tests.result` was `success`, the gate's one step (run only if `contains(needs.*.result, …)` found `failure`, `cancelled` or `skipped`) did not run, and the check passed. So a pull request could merge without its oldest-toolchain tests having run, by no one's action: a runner shortage was enough. The same held for every leg of `app-tests` (five) and `release-build` (two). | **Fixed.** `needs:` and the old step stay (the list is what makes the job wait); a second step, `if: always()`, lists every job of the run attempt (`gh api --paginate …/actions/runs/$RUN_ID/attempts/$RUN_ATTEMPT/jobs?per_page=100`) and fails unless every job but itself is `completed` with conclusion `success`, naming each that is not; a job still queued or running fails it, not waited for. It recognises itself by name (`SELF`, which a script test holds equal to the job's `name:`; no context carries a job's name) and requires itself in the listing exactly once, and it holds the names to `EXPECTED`, the twelve jobs and legs it should see, which a script test derives from the file's jobs and matrix `include`s and compares, so a leg GitHub never created, a short listing or a new job not yet in `needs:` fails it. A failed request or an empty listing fails it (`set -e`; itself missing). A re-run is judged by the new attempt's listing: GitHub's REST description does not say whether it includes jobs carried over unrun, but this run's attempt 2 (a re-run of failed jobs) listed all thirteen as attempt 2, completed and successful, and the step passed on it; if a later attempt ever listed less, the gate fails closed. Values reach the script only through `env:`; the job checks nothing out and gains `actions: read`, its one permission beyond `contents: read` (`07-supply-chain.md` §3.2). Run against that run's two real listings, the step failed attempt 1 naming the cancelled leg and passed attempt 2. `test_required_checks.py` runs the step as it is in `ci.yml` against a stand-in `gh` (18 tests: the case that happened, failed, skipped, unfinished, self, two pages, API error, empty, missing and unknown legs, a later attempt). *Not yet run on GitHub*: confirmed by the next `ci.yml` run. |

What this part of the audit did not settle. **The reader.** Every YAML audit now rests on a few hundred lines that read
a subset of YAML. Its acceptance was compared with PyYAML's over some thirty thousand mutated copies of the audited
files without a structural disagreement, and it refuses characters at which a YAML 1.1 parser starts a new line; that
is evidence, not proof, that nothing it accepts is read otherwise by GitHub or XcodeGen. **The Mac.** Z102 and Z105
change what every app job does and add a job. Z102's first run with Xcode failed closed on a wrong assumption (Xcode
writes no `Package.resolved` for this project), which is the failure it was built to have; its second version reads
the checked-out sources and has not run yet, and Z105 has not run at all, so a Release-only build error may still turn
`release-build` red on first use. Neither is confirmed until a run passes. **The settings.** Z100 and Z101 end
in repository settings and a workflow run that only the owner can make.
